# Section One.

## On the General Attributes of the Primitive and Cabbalistic Science.

---

Long before the time of Moses, there existed among the Egyptians, the Chaldeans, and the Arabs a philosophy of numbers to which they gave the magnificent name *Gabala* <note>The author uses the spelling "Gabala," likely to support the following etymological claim. Traditionally, "Kabbalah" is derived from the Hebrew root *QBL*, meaning "to receive."</note>, to indicate thereby that it was exalted above all sciences; for *Gabala* originates from the word *Gibel* <note>original: "Gibel." Likely referring to the Hebrew *Gabel* or *Giv'ah*, meaning a hill or high place, or perhaps *Gevul*, meaning a boundary or limit.</note>, which means "exalted" and signifies a height. In essence, it was a symbolic science of numbers.

Pythagoras had learned it from the Egyptians. The most famous sects of the Greeks possessed it. They were proud of it, especially the Platonists.

The master secret of this number-philosophy consisted in the fact that it reduced and concentrated all objects into a unity by means of the number Three; then from this unity, through the same number—

<meta>catchword: all</meta>

<summary>This page introduces the "Gabala" (Cabala) as an ancient, supreme "science of numbers" predating Moses and shared by Egyptians, Chaldeans, and Greeks. It defines the core of this philosophy as the reduction of all things to a unity via the number three.</summary>
<keywords>Gabala, Cabala, numerology, Pythagoras, Platonists, Egyptians, Chaldeans, number three, unity, etymology of Gabala</keywords>

<meta>catchword: It</meta>

*) The great **Leibniz** <note>Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz (1646–1716), a brilliant German philosopher and mathematician who co-invented calculus and dreamt of a universal symbolic language.</note>, convinced of the possibility of tracing all objects back to a unity and producing from it the greatest and most consistent diversity of sequences, said in his *Miscellanea*: "He would act excellently who could devise a method for changing a given sign—for example, the sign of Unity—for any given number in such a way that the sign would be exactly characteristic of the number, so that in the sign one might clearly perceive the ratio that the number has to Unity, which would happen if the sign were changed into another in the same way that Unity was changed into the number. Thus, no lack of signs for indicating numbers would be feared, and any operations could be performed without the slightest danger of error." <note>original: "Praeclare ageret, qui methodum excogitaret datam e. g. Unitatis notam pro dato quolibet numero ita commutandi, ut nota talis respectu numeri esset exacte caracteristica, adeoque ut in nota quasi distincte intueri liceret numeri rationem, quam ipse habet ad unitatem, quod tum fieret, quando eodem modo nota commutaretur in aliam, quo in numerum Unitas mutata fuit. Ita notarum pro significandis numeris defectus non esset metuendus, et ita sine tantillo errandi periculo peragi possent Operationes quaecunque."</note>

<summary>This page illustrates the "Rule of Three" as a universal law governing theology, physics, and statecraft, using a triad structure (1+1+1=3). It invokes Leibniz's theory of a universal symbolic language to justify the idea that all complexity can be reduced to a mathematical unity.</summary>
<keywords>Leibniz, Trinity, Creation, Equilibrium, Political Philosophy, Mathematical Symbolism, Universal Characteristic, Unity</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "primitive Cabalistic" science of numbers, following the previous page's introduction of how this system uses mathematical logic to categorize all sciences. The catchword from the previous page was "It," which begins this page's first sentence ("It was only natural...").</meta>

It was only natural that the philosophy of numbers had to choose general formulas, through which it brought all sciences into comprehensible divisions common to them all, in order to compare and evaluate them against one another all the more easily. The formulas had to be symbolic and so precise that, at first glance, one obtained a sufficient explanation of the subjects peculiar to each science.

Pythagoras <note>A Greek philosopher and mathematician (c. 570–495 BC) whose followers believed that "all is number."</note> became so famous among his contemporaries through this teaching, and was so honored by posterity, that long after the time of Plato, praise was sung of him in the following verses:

> With his mind he reached the gods; whatever Nature denied 
> to human sight, he drew in with the eyes of his heart. 
> <note>original Latin: "Mente Deos adiit, Quidquid Natura negavit, Visibus humanis, oculis ea pectoris hausit."</note>

The reduction of all objects to 1 by means of the number 3 was the foundation of the Pythagorean system of doctrine—or more accurately, the primitive Cabalistic <note>The author uses "Gabalistisch," a variation of Cabalistic (Kabbalah), referring to ancient Jewish mystical traditions regarding the divine nature of numbers and letters.</note> system. But to derive all diversities and eccentric modifications from the Unity through that same number 3, it was necessary for him to employ the opposite (or the oppositions), namely the number seven. From 1, the opposite was 7 — from 2, it was 8 — from 3, it was 9 — from 4, it was 10 — from 5, it was 11 — from 6, it was 12.

<sig>A 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<summary>This page explains how Pythagorean and "Cabalistic" number philosophy uses symbolic formulas to categorize all knowledge, specifically focusing on the relationship between the numbers 1, 3, and 7. It positions these numerical relationships as the foundation for understanding the diversity of the universe through mathematical "oppositions."</summary>
<keywords>number philosophy, Pythagoras, Cabala, numerology, Trinity, Law of Opposites, mathematical symbolism</keywords>

Perhaps it was for this reason that the number 7 was a sacred number, or a sacred formula, to various peoples of the East; because they used it as a key to explain all sciences with their objects and their various modifications; for one finds:
in their astronomy — 7 planets.
in their natural science — 7 metals.
— — — 7 primary colors.
— — — 7 primary tones.
in their moral philosophy — — — 7 remedies for the passions.
in their statesmanship — 7 objects of administration,
etc.

It would have been ideal if one could have also reduced the linguistic alphabet to 7 numbers. However, the sheer quantity of objects made this impossible. It was instead set at 12.

This number was called "Completeness," or the "mysterious number"; because it corresponds to the base number 3 of the system of creation, which is divided into the 4 <term>trigons<note>In historical astrology, a trigon is a group of three zodiac signs belonging to the same element (fire, earth, air, or water).</note></term> of the 12 heavenly signs, and consequently corresponds to unity:

->1 — 3 —<-
->4<-
->---<-
->7<-
->---<-
->12.<-

The variation of the <term>Δ trigons<note>original: "Δ Trigonen"; the delta symbol (Δ) represents a triangle, which in astrology refers to the "trine," a 120-degree aspect between planets.</note></term>, the sight of the oppositions, and the square and sextile <term>aspects<note>original: "gevierten, der gesechsten Scheine"; historical astrological terms for the 90-degree (square) and 60-degree (sextile) angles between celestial bodies.</note></term> provided so much material for modifications that, by reducing the objects to unity by means of the number 3, one arrived at the very unit from which they had originated through these various modifications.

The first author of the philosophy of numbers who used the number 7 as a key to provide a characteristic for all numbers rising above 10—from 11 to 99—or to make them symbolic, chose the characters of the 7 planets and divided them as can be seen here.

# Second Section.
## Causes of These Attributes.

The Ancients had an idea that numbers must be <term>sympathetic</term> <note>possessing a hidden natural connection or mystical affinity</note>, because the number 9, which closes the first <term>decade</term> <note>a group of ten; here referring to the return to a zero-ending number like 10</note> from 0 to 0, has the special property that, no matter what number you multiply it by, it produces a result of two digits which, when added together, produce the number nine again; for example:

| Multiplier | | Number | | Result | Digits added |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 9 | multiplied by | 2 | gives | 18 | (1+8 = 9) |
| 9 | — | 3 | — | 27 | (2+7 = 9) |
| 9 | — | 4 | — | 36 | (3+6 = 9) |
| 9 | — | 5 | — | 45 | (4+5 = 9) |
| 9 | — | 6 | — | 54 | (5+4 = 9) |
| 9 | — | 7 | — | 63 | (6+3 = 9) |
| 9 | — | 8 | — | 72 | (7+2 = 9) |
| 9 | — | 9 | — | 81 | (8+1 = 9) |

Likewise in addition:

| First Number | | Added to | | Result |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 1 | | 8 | — | 9 |
| 2 | | 7 | — | 9 |
| 3 | | 6 | — | 9 |
| 4 | | 5 | — | 9 |
| 5 | | 4 | — | 9 |
| 6 | | 3 | — | 9 |
| 7 | | 2 | — | 9 |
| 8 | | 1 | — | 9 |

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<summary>This page explains the mathematical rationale behind "number sympathy," specifically focusing on the unique digital root properties of the number 9. It demonstrates how 9 consistently reproduces itself through multiplication and addition, which ancient scholars viewed as evidence of mystical significance.</summary>

<keywords>number sympathy, decade, number 9, multiplication properties, addition properties, sacred mathematics</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on numerical reduction and "sympathy," transitioning from pure mathematics to the historical and astronomical origins of these systems as attributed to ancient Egyptian observations.</meta>

There is no other means of finding the <term>sympathy of numbers</term> <note>a mystical or natural correspondence between numerical values and physical reality</note> than to concentrate them and reduce them to units—that is, from 1 to 9—as has been demonstrated here in the addition.

If one observes the numbers of each <term>planetary character</term> <note>symbolic or numerical representations assigned to the planets</note> shown on the first table and adds each one individually, one becomes aware of the progression used by the original creator of this arithmetic game to determine the sympathy of numbers in this specific way and no other.

Once the Egyptians, after many years of observation and calculation, had reached the point where they could specify the periodic orbit of the planets around the sun, their distance from the earth, the difference in their diameters, and consequently their size and solidity, they examined the weather conditions observed according to their orbits—or rather their <term>orbital stations</term> <note>specific points or positions in a planet's path</note>—in relation to the four <term>trigons</term> <note>groups of three zodiac signs belonging to the same element; here used to classify the seasons</note> of the twelve heavenly signs. They used this name to designate the four seasons.

->Spring. Summer. Autumn and Winter.<-

They found that spring warms that which had grown cold;
summer ripens and dries that which grows through warming;
autumn brings decay through moisture;
winter freezes, chills, and places all into a state of <term>inertia</term> <note>from the Latin 'inertia,' referring here to a state of total inactivity or physical rest in nature</note>.

<sig>A 5</sig>
<meta>catchword: They</meta>

They thus named the trigons <note>Groups of three signs of the zodiac, also known as "triplicities," each associated with an element.</note> of the 12 signs of the zodiac

| | | |
| :--- | :---: | :--- |
| the first | ♈ ♌ ♐ | Warm |
| the second | ♉ ♍ ♑ | Dry |
| the third | ♊ ♎ ♒ | Moist |
| the fourth | ♋ ♏ ♓ | Cold. |

The consequences of these effects they called
->Cholera<- <note>Refers to the "choleric" temperament, traditionally associated with fire and a personality that is ambitious or easily angered.</note>
->Melancholy<- <note>The "melancholic" temperament, associated with earth and a pensive or despondent personality.</note>
->Blood<- <note>The "sanguine" temperament, associated with air and a lively, social personality.</note>
->Phlegm.<- <note>The "phlegmatic" temperament, associated with water and a calm, stoic personality.</note>

Consequently, the actions that arise from such qualities.
->Violence<-
->Slowness<-
->Quickness<-
->Inertia.<-

The trigons, in which the 7 planets show their aspects <note>In astrology, an "aspect" is the angle planets make to each other in the zodiac, believed to influence their power.</note> and each of them performs its revolution, naturally led them to the idea that they should assign certain individual signs of the zodiac to the planets as <term>favorite stations</term> <note>These are likely "houses" or "exaltations," specific signs where a planet's influence is considered strongest.</note> corresponding to their slow or fast movement and the resulting effects. Therefore, it happened that they also attributed the following properties to the planets.

| | | |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| ♄ | — Aratron — | cold and dry |
| ♃ | — Petor — | moist and warm |
| ♂ | — Phalee — | warm and dry |
| ☉ | — Och — | warm and dry |
| ♀ | — Hagith — | moist and warm |
| ☿ | — Ophiel — | according to the nature of its neighbors |
| ☾ | — Phul — | cold and moist. |
<note>The names listed next to the planets (Aratron, Petor, etc.) are the seven "Olympic Spirits" famously described in the Arbatel, a 16th-century book of magic. Each spirit rules over one of the seven planetary spheres.</note>

<page-num>11</page-num>

They judged these very same qualities in the elements:
Earth 🜃 dry 🜃 cold
Water 🜄 moist 🜄 cold
Air ✳ moist ✳ warm
Fire 🜂 dry 🜂 warm.

Always proceeding in this division of four, they subdivided the Zodiac into the four <term>triplicities</term> <note>From the Latin *triplicitas*; a grouping of three zodiac signs belonging to the same element.</note> or trigons — Fire — Water — Air — Earth. They called the first trigon the fiery one, namely ♈ ♌ ♐ <note>Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius.</note>. The second, the Earthly one ♉ ♍ ♑ <note>Taurus, Virgo, and Capricorn.</note>. The third, the Airy one ♊ ♎ ♒ <note>Gemini, Libra, and Aquarius.</note>. The fourth, the Watery one ♋ ♏ ♓ <note>Cancer, Scorpio, and Pisces.</note>; because they formed triangles in the figure.

<image-desc>A complex astrological diagram representing the twelve signs of the Zodiac and their associated elements (the Triplicities). At the center is a double-ringed circle with radiating sunburst lines, enclosed in a square labeled with the letters 'A' and 'B'. This central structure is overlaid by four interlocking triangles forming a star pattern. Outside the points of the star are the symbols for the twelve signs: ♈ (Aries), ♉ (Taurus), ♊ (Gemini), ♋ (Cancer), ♌ (Leo), ♍ (Virgo), ♎ (Libra), ♏ (Scorpio), ♐ (Sagittarius), ♑ (Capricorn), ♒ (Aquarius), and ♓ (Pisces). The internal triangular segments are labeled with the elements: "Feurig" (Fire), "Irdisch" (Earth), "Luftig" (Air), and "Wäßrig" (Water), repeated in sequence to show the elemental classification of each sign. A small signature "J. F. Wetzel" appears in the lower left portion of the diagram.</image-desc>

<meta>Continuing from page 11, which established the elemental qualities (dry, cold, moist, warm) and their initial assignment to the zodiacal trigons. The arithmetic order of the **Trigons** <note>Also called triplicities; groups of three zodiac signs belonging to the same element.</note> of the heavenly signs was consequently this:

| | in Signs | | in Names |
|:---|:---|:---:|:---|
| Fiery | 1. 5. 9 — | ♈ ♌ ♐ | Aries, Leo, Sagittarius. |
| Earthy | 2. 6. 10 — | ♉ ♍ ♑ | Taurus, Virgo, Capricorn |
| Airy | 3. 7. 11 — | ♊ ♎ ♒ | Gemini, Libra, Aquarius |
| Watery | 4. 8. 12 — | ♋ ♏ ♓ | Cancer, Scorpio, Pisces. |

These triplicities must have been especially striking to them, because they had observed that the two most distant and largest planets, **Saturn** (♄) and **Jupiter** (♃), on their long journey through the **Zodiac**, always performed their **conjunctions** <note>Meetings where planets appear at the same point in the sky.</note> and **oppositions** <note>Alignments where planets are on opposite sides of the Earth.</note> within one and the same triplicity for two hundred full years (without occurring periodically in another). They then passed from that one into another, in order to mark out their conjunctions and oppositions in that one also for 200 years; likewise into the third, to do the same, and finally into the fourth, so that from there—after the completed number of conjunctions and oppositions—they would meet again at the same point of the Zodiac where they had started.

To complete all four triplicities with their periodic conjunctions and oppositions, they (these two planets) required a full 800 years. The eight-hundredth year, calculated from their first meeting, was called the ***great year*** <note>original: "annus magnus"</note>, and in the astronomy of the ancients, the ***great revolution*** <note>original: "Revolutio magna"</note>, or the "cycle of the greater ones."

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 11 regarding the astronomical cycles of Saturn and Jupiter. ->——— ❦ ——— 13<-

Eight hundred years, or the revolution through four **trigons** <note>Groups of three zodiac signs belonging to the same element.</note> of these **conjunctions** <note>Meetings where planets appear at the same point in the sky.</note> and **oppositions** <note>Alignments where planets are on opposite sides of the Earth.</note>, were called by the **Platonists** <note>Followers of the philosophy of Plato, who often sought mathematical harmony in the cosmos.</note> the ***Greatest Time*** <note>original: "Tempus maximum"</note>—

Four hundred years, or the revolution through two trigons, was called the ***Middle Time*** <note>original: "Tempus medium"</note>—

A quarter of the revolution, or the conjunctions and oppositions in a single trigon, was called the ***Least Time*** <note>original: "Tempus minimum"</note>.

The observations made in **meteorology** by the **Egyptians** showed that between 18 and 19 years—namely, shortly before a conjunction or opposition of **Saturn** and **Jupiter**—almost the same weather conditions recur and the same winds appear. This prompted them even more to precisely understand the system of meetings and oppositions between Saturn and Jupiter. Because of this meteorological study, the view of these conjunctions and oppositions of Saturn and Jupiter is also worthy of our attention; I present an essay on this to my worthy readers at the end of this work.

Many years of observation had fully convinced the ancient Egyptians that Saturn, because of its slow pace, requires nearly 30 years to wander through the 12 signs of the **Zodiac**. Jupiter, however, requires 12 years to complete the same path; consequently, in a period of 30 years, it has already completed its circuit two and a half times <note>The original German "dritthalbmal" is an archaic expression meaning "half of the third," or 2.5.</note> before Saturn comes around once. In the time that Jupiter wanders through them,

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion from page 13 regarding the astronomical relationship between Saturn and Jupiter, specifically their meeting points (conjunctions) and opposite points (oppositions) within the zodiac.</meta>

...travels, Saturn only covers 5 signs, and before he has visited 3 of these, Jupiter has already moved 8 signs further. Thus, in the twentieth year, Jupiter meets again with Saturn in a sign of the **Trigon** <note>The symbol △ refers to a "Trigon," a grouping of three zodiac signs associated with one of the four elements; here it represents the "Great Mutation" cycle where conjunctions stay within signs of the same element for about 200 years.</note>, which serves as their meeting hall for conjunctions and oppositions for 200 years. Therefore, every 20 years, an opposition or conjunction arises; for example, in the **Year of the World** <note>original: "Jahre der Welt" — also known as *Anno Mundi*, a calendar era based on the biblical account of the creation of the world.</note> 3918 a conjunction occurs, 10 years after that an opposition, 10 years after that a conjunction again, and then in the following 10th year another opposition.

In the precise calculation, these cycles take 19 years, 313 days, 16 minutes, 13 seconds, and 49 thirds of a day; consequently, the cycle falls just 52 days short of a full 20 years.

The distance from one opposition in the **Zodiac** <term>Zodiac: the "circle of animals," the belt of constellations through which the sun and planets appear to move.</term> is 8 signs, 2 degrees, 58 minutes, 11 seconds, and 29 thirds. These regular occurrences (for we are not speaking of the extraordinary ones) always fall exactly midway between two regular conjunctions.

Extraordinary conjunctions of both planets, which might repeat several times within a single month or year <note>This refers to "apparent retrograde motion," where planets seem to move backward and pass each other multiple times due to Earth's own orbit.</note>, are not considered here; we focus only on the twenty-year cycles.

The great revolution of both planets, which occurs every eight hundred years—specifically returning to the exact point where the first one occurred—amounts according to precise calculation to:
->794 years, 48 minutes, 12 seconds, and 28 thirds of a day.<-

<meta>This page continues the technical mathematical explanation of the orbital cycles of Saturn and Jupiter. The <term>progression</term> <note>The shifting movement of the points where planets meet relative to the fixed stars/degrees of the zodiac.</note> of the <term>conjunctions</term> <note>Conjunction: When two planets align at the same point in the sky.</note> in degrees is always, for every 3 degrees, a little less than 2 minutes.

After every seventh <term>conjunction</term>, a change in the calculation arises, so that at the seventh, instead of 20 years, only 19 must be reckoned; and because there is a lack of 26 days in the distance between each <term>conjunction</term> and <term>opposition</term> <note>Opposition: When two planets are on opposite sides of the Earth, 180 degrees apart.</note> when calculated at 10 years; it follows that after ninety <term>conjunctions</term>, a ninety-first must follow in the same <term>Trigon</term> <note>Trigon: A group of three zodiac signs belonging to the same element; these planets meet within signs of the same element for approximately 200 years before moving to the next element.</note>.

Both because of the aforementioned meteorological observations and for knowledge of the sublime <term>calculus</term> <note>Calculus: A systematic method of astronomical computation or the "math of the heavens."</note> of the universe, I have placed at the end of this treatise a table of all <term>conjunctions</term> of Saturn and Jupiter from the beginning of the world until our own, and even somewhat later, times.

According to the expression: "great year" <note>original: "annus magnus" — a concept where a "year" is measured by the time it takes for all planets to return to a specific alignment, here referring to the 800-year cycle of Saturn and Jupiter.</note>, which was attributed to the <term>revolution</term> <note>Revolution: The completion of a full orbital cycle or periodic return to a starting point.</note> of both planets after 800 years, it would therefore be no absurdity to say that the terrestrial globe upon which we live is currently only 7 1/4 years old <note>The author is calculating based on a biblical age of the world (approx. 5,800 years); 5,800 divided by the 800-year "Great Year" equals roughly 7.25.</note>.

Even to modern astronomers, the sublime order of the greater <term>conjunctions</term> and <term>oppositions</term> of Saturn and Jupiter is a majestic spectacle.

> Jupiter's protection, shining back against impious Saturn, rescued you and slowed the wings of swift fate — <note>original: "Te Iovis impio tutela Saturno Refulgens, eripuit volucrisque fati Tardavit alas" — from Horace’s Odes (2.17). <meta>This page continues the historical application of the astrological theory introduced on page 17. The Persian monarchy took its beginning during an opposition of Saturn and Jupiter, when the former was in Virgo and the latter in a house of Saturn, in Pisces <note>original: ♓</note>. This constellation, they said, signifies a short duration. The Persian monarchy indeed lasted only 200 years; it only reached the <term>square</term><note>An astrological aspect where two planets are 90 degrees apart, usually indicating a period of crisis or challenge.</note> and perished in the airy <term>trigon</term><note>A group of three zodiac signs belonging to the element of Air (Gemini, Libra, Aquarius).</note> when Alexander overcame the last Darius.

The Macedonian or Greek monarchy took its beginning in this airy trigon—the events of which were esteemed to be of short duration—and likewise lasted no more than 300 years; it reached its end with the victory that Pompey gained over King Perseus in the watery trigon.

The ancient Roman state took its beginning during the third opposition of Saturn and Jupiter in the fifth fiery trigon. It was then that Rome was built by Romulus. In the fifth earthy trigon, this state received a changed form of government, and it became a republic. In the fifth watery trigon, the republican constitution was overthrown when Julius Caesar defeated the Pompeians. The state gradually became a monarchy. In the sixth fiery trigon <note>original: △</note>, it was divided into the Western and Eastern Empires. In the sixth airy trigon <note>original: △</note>, the Western part perished, and in the seventh watery trigon, the Eastern part of the same met its end. Generally, the fiery trigons were very favorable to this state—in conquests and its enlargement—but the earthy ones through ni- <note>The text cuts off here at "Nie-", likely leading to "Niederlagen" (defeats) or "Niedergang" (decline) on the following page.</note>

One paid attention to those planets and stars that are closest to them in a <term>conjunction<gloss>an alignment where two celestial bodies appear close together in the sky</gloss></term> or <term>opposition<gloss>an alignment where two celestial bodies are on opposite sides of the Earth</gloss></term>, and their properties were investigated. For example, Mars <note>symbol: ♂</note> in the signs of Aries <note>symbol: ♈</note>, Leo <note>symbol: ♌</note>, and Sagittarius <note>symbol: ♐</note> pointed to drought or dry years. As proof of this is the conjunction of Saturn <note>symbol: ♄</note> and Jupiter <note>symbol: ♃</note> that occurred in 1782 in Sagittarius, which was preceded by a very hot summer as a forerunner. But if the nearest planet is Venus <note>symbol: ♀</note> or the Moon <note>symbol: ☾</note> in the signs of Pisces <note>symbol: ♓</note>, Scorpio <note>symbol: ♏</note>, or Cancer <note>symbol: ♋</note>, then great wetness, foul air <note>often referring to "miasma," which was historically believed to spread disease</note>, and a cooling of the earth will precede the conjunction or opposition.

If Mars stands in a fiery sign or near one, then terrible thunder and hailstorms result. If Mercury <note>symbol: ☿</note> is near them in an earthly sign, earthquakes follow; if he is near them in an airy sign, then storm winds come.

If Mars and Mercury meet near an opposition, then murders, thefts, and betrayals occur.

If Mars and Mercury are near an opposition, there are many unfortunate deliveries and many premature births.

The scholars applied themselves to observing the effects that the temperature of the air—at or between the oppositions of Saturn and Jupiter—had on individual humans, on animals, and on plants, depending on whether such occurred in the fiery, the earthly, the airy, or the watery <term>trigons<gloss>groups of three zodiac signs belonging to the same element</gloss></term>, with the difference in sensation among <term>choleric, melancholic, sanguine, and phlegmatic<gloss>the four 'humors' or temperaments once thought to dictate human health and personality</gloss></term> constitutions.

**Sanguine**: Associated with air/blood; tending toward optimism and social warmth.

<summary>This page serves as a reference chart linking alchemical elements and celestial symbols to the four human temperaments and their physical qualities (hot, cold, moist, dry). It visualizes the core Hermetic principle of the connection between the physical body, the soul, and the elemental world.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, four humors, spirit, soul, body, choleric, phlegmatic, melancholic, sanguine, elements, temperaments</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>Continuing the discussion from the previous page regarding the assignment of symbolic numbers to the planets based on their perceived qualities.</meta>

The same research led them to knowledge of <term>Uranology</term> (the study of the air and heavens), <term>Geology</term> (the study of the earth), <term>Lithology</term> (the study of stones), <term>Hydrology</term> (the study of waters), <term>Pyrology</term> (the study of fire), <term>Botany</term> (the study of plants), <term>Anthropology</term> (the study of mankind), <term>Ornithology</term> (the study of birds), <term>Quadrupedology</term> (the study of four-footed animals), <term>Ichthyology</term> <note>original: "Iztiologie"</note> (the study of fish), and <term>Chemistry</term>.

Wherever they found cold, dry properties, they believed these deserved to be assigned to Saturn <note>represented by the symbol ♄</note>.

Moist, warm properties were assigned to Jupiter <note>represented by the symbol ♃</note>.

Hot and dry properties to Mars <note>represented by the symbol ♂</note>, and so on for the remaining five planets <note>Historically, this includes the Sun and Moon</note> which harmonized with the temperatures already described above.

The various modifications of these types of temperature gave cause to assign them special symbolic numbers. These were allocated from 11 to 99 to the seven planets, as we have already shown before, undoubtedly with the intention of assisting the student in the <term>Qabalistic</term> <note>original: "gabalistischen"</note> science through the use of "local memory" <note>The "method of loci," a mnemonic technique where one associates information with specific physical locations</note>.

A "sympathy" <note>In Hermetic philosophy, "sympathy" refers to the hidden cosmic connections between physical objects and celestial bodies</note> was attributed to these numbers, of which a special study also exists.

First, we wish to present the symbolic number theory in tables. It follows here.

<summary>This page lists the scientific disciplines categorized by early researchers and explains how planetary associations (Saturn, Jupiter, Mars) were determined by physical qualities like heat and moisture. It also introduces the use of symbolic numbers (11-99) as mnemonic devices in Qabalistic studies.</summary>
<keywords>Uranology, Saturn, Jupiter, Mars, Qabala, mnemonics, planetary temperament, symbolic numbers, occult sciences</keywords>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>Page 23. This page continues the discussion of symbolic numerology following the illegible tables on page 22. Catchword: Mes. Signature: B 4.</meta>

These were the general abstractions regarding the meanings of numbers.

Now we move to the meanings they were intended to have in the various branches of natural science; namely:

The meaning of metals and minerals—colors—scent—taste—places and regions—animals—human temperaments—professions and the accidents of human life—human diseases and their cure—the works of chemistry—the industrial arts—the sublime sciences—hidden things.

The reason why no number below 11 and none above 99 was made symbolic was presumably this: because, as we will show further below, one only selected 3 numbers standing in a <term>trigon</term> <note>original: "Trigon"; in astrology, this refers to a triangular relationship between signs or numbers, often associated with the four elements</note>, and concentrated their sum so that it produced a <term>sympathetic number</term> <note>A number believed to have a mystical resonance or "sympathy" with a celestial body or natural force</note>, which indicated the planet that was to provide the revelation.

For example: the trigon might contain the numbers 25, 45, and 64. Summed together, they give the number 134; this was called the **Key**. Now one "concentrated" <note>A numerological process of reducing large numbers to single or double digits through addition</note> them and said: 4 and 3 make 7; 3 and 1 make 4; together 47. Thus, 47 is the **harmonic** number which, when concentrated once more, gives 11 as a **sympathetic** number—and if one proceeds in the same way with it, gives 2 as a **planetary number**, specifically that of Jupiter, which signifies acquisition.

The following tables provide specific meanings.

<summary>This page explains the application of symbolic numbers to various fields of natural science and details the numerological calculations used to derive "planetary numbers" from a "trigon."</summary>
<keywords>numerology, natural science, trigon, sympathetic numbers, Jupiter, planetary numbers, harmonic numbers, divination</keywords>

## Third Section.

---

->Now the teachers of the <term>Gabalic science<gloss>A variant of "Kabbalistic," referring here to a tradition of mystical or occult knowledge.</gloss></term> proceed<-
->to the explanation of the<-

### Meanings
->of the<-
# twelve houses of their <term>chart<gloss>Original: "Thema," meaning an astrological birth chart or horoscope.</gloss></term>.

---

->They were contained in the following two Latin<-
->verses.<-

->1. 2. 3. 4. 5. 6.<-
->Life, Profit, Brothers, Father, Offspring, Health.<-
<note>original: "Vita, Lucrum, Fratres, Genitor, Nati, Valetudo."</note>
->7. 8. 9. 10. 11. 12.<-
->Wife, Death, Wisdom, Ruling, Benefits, Spirit.<-
<note>original: "Uxór, Mors, Sapiens, Regnans, Benefactaque, Dæmon."</note>

1. **House: Life**, *Life*, <note>original: "Vita"</note> indicates the physical constitution, health circumstances, the character of the mind, pleasure, displeasure, fortune, misfortune, and lifespan.

2. **House: Profit**, *Profit*, <note>original: "Lucrum"</note> indicates: wealth, poverty, gain, loss in trade, gambling and betting, livestock, and movable property.

<meta>catchword: 3. House</meta>

<summary>This page introduces the Third Section of the text, defining the traditional meanings of the twelve houses in a horoscope, beginning with the first two: Life and Profit.</summary>

3. **H. Relatives, Brothers**, <note>Latin: *Fratres*</note> indicates: friendship or enmity, favor or disfavor from them, help or hindrance from them, their well-being or illness, etc.

4. **H. Parents, Father**, <note>Latin: *Genitor*</note> indicates their well-being or illness, their love or disgrace, their good or bad circumstances; also inheritance, landed property, gardens, buildings, and treasures buried under the earth.

5. **H. Children** } **Descendants**, <note>Latin: *Nati*</note> indicates: love, preg-
   **or**           } nancy, the delivery of women, the
   **Love,**        } number and gender of children;
   love affairs, the chastity or unchastity of persons; also messages, letters, and servants.

6. **H. Sickness, Health**, <note>Latin: *Valetudo*</note> indicates: the type, circumstance, and turn of the sickness. Also thefts, the recovery or non-recovery of stolen items. The appearance and location of the thief.

7. **H. Wives** } **Wife**, <note>Latin: *Uxor*</note> indicates: what concerns the
   **and**      } state of marriage, and also what
   **Enemies,**  } hostile things are encountered. One's victory over enemies, or their victory over oneself.

8. **H. Death, Death**, <note>Latin: *Mors*</note> indicates: both the end of man and the outcome of all business—

<meta>This page continues the instructional sequence for apprentices, moving from the astrological houses discussed on page 111 to the linguistic and numerical systems of the "Kabbalistic Alphabet."</meta>

## Fourth Section.

---

### After this knowledge, they presented the
# Kabbalistic Alphabet
### to the apprentices.

---

Just as they had concentrated the numbers from 11 to 99 and arranged them under the 7 planets, they also found it necessary to reduce the spoken alphabet to 12 consonants. They left out the vowels and arranged it thus:

| b | c | d | f | g | l | m | n | r | s | x | y |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| p | k | t | v | h | | | | | z | chs | z |
| w | q | | | ch | | | | | | | |

They assigned a number to each of these consonants. The numbers they chose for this purpose were as follows:

| 10 | 15 | 20 | 25 | 30 | 35 | 40 | 45 | 50 | 55 | 60 | 65 |
|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|

The Kabbalistic script <note>original: "gabalistische Schrift"</note> was consequently written without vowels; the person reading it had to mentally supply the vowel sounds <note>original: "Selbstlauter", literally "self-sounders", a common German term for vowels</note>.

<meta>catchword: To</meta>

<summary>This page describes a "Kabbalistic Alphabet" taught to apprentices, which reduces the alphabet to twelve primary consonant groups and assigns each a numerical value in increments of five. It emphasizes a system where vowels are omitted in writing and must be supplied by the reader, similar to ancient Semitic scripts.</summary>

<keywords>Kabbalistic alphabet, numerology, consonants, vowels, apprentices, linguistic reduction, phonetic grouping</keywords>

--- Page 121 ---
<meta>This page continues the explanation of the Kabbalistic script, specifically focusing on its historical origins and the practical application of the numerical system for formulating questions.</meta>

In the times of the Romans, when Cicero lived, this method of writing was called the Tironian style, because Tiro <note>Marcus Tullius Tiro (d. 4 BC), the slave and later freedman of the orator Cicero, who is credited with inventing the first standardized Latin shorthand system</note>, Cicero's bondman and secretary, used it to write as quickly as his master dictated. For example, Cicero dictated: *If you are well, it is good; I, indeed, am well* <note>original Latin: "Si vales bene est, ego quidem valeo"</note>. Tiro wrote it down thus: *S vls bn st g qdm vl*.

The Gabalists <note>original: "Gabalisten," a phonetic variant of Kabbalists</note> wrote all their questions in this same way.

## Drafting the Questions.

Their rule was that all possible questions, regarding human affairs and events as well as general occurrences and scientific subjects, had to be composed as briefly as possible and defined as clearly as possible, while everything non-essential had to be omitted.

->e.g., What age will N. N. Sturmfeder<-
->reach?<-

This question was written thus:

| W. — 10 | l — 35 | W — 10 | — — | S — 55 |
|:---|:---|:---|:---|:---|
| l — 35 | t — 20 | r — 50 | — — | t — 20 |
| ch — 30 | r — 50 | d — 20 | — — | r — 50 |
| | | r — 50 | — — | m — 40 |
| | | r — 50 | | f — 25 |
| | | ch — 30 | | d — 20 |
| | | n — 45 | | r — 50 |
| **75** | **105** | **255** | **160** | **265** |

<sig>H</sig>
<meta>catchword: Then</meta>

<summary>This page links the "Gabalistic" shorthand to Roman Tironian notes and demonstrates how to condense a question into its consonant components to calculate its numerical sum.</summary>
<keywords>Tironian notes, shorthand, Cicero, Marcus Tullius Tiro, Gabalists, gematria, Sturmfeder, linguistic reduction</keywords>

--- Page 122 ---
<meta>This page continues the explanation of the Kabbalistic method of shorthand and numerology. On the previous page, a specific question about a person named Sturmfeder was coded into numbers. This page details how those numbers are totaled and arranged into a grid.</meta>

Then they combined the obtained amounts into a grand total <term>Hauptsumme|The final numerical value derived from adding up the individual values assigned to the letters in the abbreviated question.</term>.

->75<-
->105<-
->255<-
->160<-
->265<-
->---<-
->860<-

The grand total of the entire question now became the basis of a square <note>The original German uses "Viertel" (quarter), referring to a four-by-four grid.</note>, which consisted of 16 compartments. In the bottom four, they arranged the grand total in reverse order, namely:

| A | G | H | B |
|---|---|---|---|
| E | L | M | F |
| C | I | K | D |
| <unclear>1</unclear> | 8 | 6 | 1 |

Note. In their symbolic numbers, as was seen above, there is no zero. Whenever their result produced a zero, they converted it into the number 1. <note>This explains why the total 860 is represented in the table as 1, 8, 6, 1. The zero became a 1, and an additional 1 was likely used as a filler for the fourth cell.</note> —

In the case of long questions, it happens that the grand total yields four digits. They then placed the thousands-digit in the first compartment of the base row. Because this compartment must also be filled in cases where the grand total consists of only three digits,

<vocab>Grand total, Square, Compartments, In reverse order, Symbolic numbers, Zero, Thousands-digit, Base row</vocab>

<summary>This page explains how the numerical values from a coded question are summed and entered into the bottom row of a 16-cell grid in reverse order. It specifically notes the Kabbalistic practice of replacing the number zero with one.</summary>
<keywords>Kabbalah, numerology, grand total, 16-cell grid, zero substitution, calculation, base row</keywords>

--- Page 123 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical instructions for filling the Kabbalistic grid. The previous page ended with "Because this compartment must also be filled in cases where the grand total consists of only three digits,". This page concludes that sentence with the word "must" (muß).</meta>

...must, they halved the hundred-digit <term>Hundertzahl|The digit in the hundreds place of a numerical value.</term> if it was an even number, or if it was an odd number, they took the number 1. In the above example, the visual appearance was as follows:

---

<image-desc>A rectangular grid diagram divided into sixteen cells (4 columns by 4 rows). The first row contains the letters A, G, H, and B. The second row contains the letters E, L, M, and F. The third row contains the letters C, I, K, and D. The fourth row contains the numbers 4, 8, 6, and 1.</image-desc>

| A | G | H | B |

# Fifth Section.
## Filling the Square.

This filling was performed by extracting square roots <term>Quadratwurzelzahlen|The results of calculating a square root from a larger number.</term> from the main parts of the question. The rule was as follows: 1. Into compartment A went the square root of the sum produced by the common or distinctive name <term>Lauf- oder Distinktname|A specific name or identifying title used for the person in this system, likely the first name.</term> of the person who is the subject of the question.

First Note. Each compartment can only contain a single digit; therefore, if the family name <term>Familienname|In this context, the surname or secondary name used for the numerical calculation.</term> has three digits, one must cut off the first two from the last one, and where it has four digits, the first two from the last two. One then extracts the square root from the two digits that were set apart. For example: In the question presented above, we have "Joseph." This family name has a total sum of 160. One <note>original: "Man", the catchword "schnei-" from the bottom of the page indicates the next page begins with "schneidet" (cuts).</note>

To present a complete answer, 24 such alphabetical numbers <note>numbers corresponding to specific letters in the system's cipher</note> were required: In addition to the 12 that had been obtained in the prescribed manner, another 12 had to be extracted, and they were drawn from those by means of a very easy operation <note>mathematical manipulation of the values</note>, namely: Plus 5 — Minus 5 or 10, Plus 10, and Plus 20. It matters little to which row one assigns such a Plus or Minus. For the true rule of its use was this: that one provided the consonants with their true harmony <note>likely referring to making the resulting word pronounceable or aesthetically pleasing</note>. If three standing next to each other were from the lowest class of the alphabet <note>the group of letters with the lowest numerical values</note>, then one placed the Plus 20 next to them to raise the extracted ones into the middle or highest class. If they were from the highest, then one applied Minus 5 or Minus 10 to them. It was the same if one found them mixed. Yet it was not permitted to use other numbers for Plus and Minus than the aforementioned 5, 10, and 20, nor to omit any of them. Where a consonant repeats itself twice in the result, it is permitted to increase it by 10.

### Insertion of the Vowels.
<note>This section reveals the final answer to the lottery question by adding vowels to the previous consonants. The resulting text is in Italian.</note>

#### First Line.
Already the net is cast, and it makes for a bad game.
<note>original: "Gia il giacchio e gettato, e fa cattivo gioco." A "giacchio" is a circular fishing net; the phrase is a variation of "the die is cast."</note>

#### Second Line.
A good custom would be to have thirty-one, thirty-three, thirty-three with one.
<note>original: "Una bona Usanza farebbe d’ auer trenta un, trenta tre, trenta tre con un." These likely refer to specific lottery numbers or combinations.</note>

#### Third Line.
It happens that you [seek] help from joy [or] appetite.
<note>original: "Decade che voi da gioja ajutto appetito." This sentence is somewhat ungrammatical, common in oracular or decoded texts.</note>

#### Fourth Line.
Dance, turn whoever wants to turn, whoever wants to dance.
<note>original: "Balla, gira chi uol girare, chi uol ballare."</note>

### Transposition of Consonants. <note>This exercise demonstrates a cryptographic method where vowels are removed, a technique often used in esoteric traditions to find "hidden" meanings or to mimic the structure of the Hebrew language, which is written primarily with consonants.</note>
---
#### First Line.
Ll. q det mn ntm t gntm nt D n ddet. <note>These are the consonants for the first Latin sentence in the second column.</note>
---
#### Second Line.
Q qd ll mn bn dbt, ngn gnvt.
---
#### Third Line.
Q dl nn clt, lg dvn ne ddet.
---
#### Fourth Line.
Prrs Lcs fl, ſc fls prpr.
---

<column-break/>

### Insertion of Vowels. <note>This column serves as the "key" or solution to the previous one, restoring the vowels to the Latin phrases.</note>
---
#### First Line.
She who judged all beginnings of beings and nations to be derived from the one God. <note>original: "Illa quæ judicavit omnia entium & gentium initia a Deo uno deducta."</note>
---
#### Second Line.
She who, because she owed all good things to Him, frankly acknowledged it. <note>original: "Quæ quod illi omnia bona debuit, ingenue agnovit."</note>
---
#### Third Line.
She who did not worship idols, devoted solely to the divine law. <note>original: "Quæ idola non coluit, legi divinæ unice addicta."</note>
---
#### Fourth Line.
Daughter of a purer Light, belonging to the sons of Isaac. <note>original: "Purioris Lucis Filia, Iſaaci filiis propria."</note>
---

## Insertion of Vowels.

---

### First Line.
Linnaeus <note>original: "Linnee," referring to Carl Linnaeus, the famous Swedish botanist and zoologist who formalized binomial nomenclature.</note> knew and named him.

### Second Line.
Waxwing. <note>original: "Seidenschwanz," literally "Silk-tail." Specifically the Bohemian Waxwing (*Bombycilla garrulus*).</note> He follows the rowan berries <note>original: "Eschenbeern," referring to the berries of the mountain ash or Rowan tree.</note> in the autumn — does not tolerate hot weather — he trills — his tone ends with a hiss.

### Third Line.
Bright yellow strings <note>Referring to the distinctive yellow tips on the tail feathers.</note> seem to beautify his black tail.

### Fourth Line.
A unique genus, which is close to the nature of the <term>fieldfares</term> <note>original: "Grametten," a dialect variation of *Krammetsvögel*, a traditional name for thrushes, particularly the Fieldfare.</note>.

### Transposition of the Consonants.
<note>In this context, "transposition" refers to the removal of vowels and the occasional substitution of specific consonants to obscure the text.</note>

### Insertion of the Vowels.

**First Line.**
This land was meant to be & must be moistened like a garden.

**Second Line.**
Name the high mountain a fountain.

**Third Line.**
The water, which will flow from it, will make a broad river; it will provide.

**Fourth Line.**
Ceaselessly along the way a rich nourishment, a desired watering for the lands of this garden.

## Insertion of the Vowels.
---
**First Line.**

Look at the mountainous region of Zion <note>Referring to the location of the Temple in Jerusalem.</note>: damaging thunderstorms break out there every year.

**Second Line.**

A gentleman, who had studied nature from its foundations, devised the advantage of conducting the lightning bolt. <note>This "gentleman" likely refers to Solomon, suggesting he was a proto-scientist who understood electrical conduction.</note>

**Third Line.**

On the roof covered with fine gold. <note>Historical accounts of Solomon’s Temple describe a gold-plated roof with sharp points, which some 18th-century writers argued acted as a lightning rod system.</note>

### Insertion of the Vowels.

**First Line.**
<note>original: "Structura cujuſque nervi eſt, ut aura ineat, exeat & varios tonos edat Aura & c."</note>
The structure of every nerve is such that the breath may enter, exit, and give forth various tones. The breath, etc. <note>The author uses "aura" (air or breath) to explain how physiological structures produce sound or life force.</note>

**Second Line.**
<note>original: "Venti cognitio, indagine tua digna effectu tonite, docebit."</note>
Knowledge of the wind, worthy of your investigation through the effect of thunder, will teach you. <note>The Latin "tonite" likely refers to "tonitru" (thunder) or the act of thundering.</note>

**Third Line.**
<note>original: "Varias paſſiones animalium, amorem Iram luſum, illuſionem ſerenam viſionem."</note>
The various passions of animals: love, anger, play, illusion, and peaceful vision.

**Fourth Line.**
<note>original: "Moerorem, gaudium, gratumque humorem."</note>
Sorrow, joy, and a pleasing <term>humor<gloss>disposition or temperament</gloss></term>. <note>In the 17th century, "humors" were bodily fluids believed to control a person's health and emotions.</note>

<meta>catchword: Zwey- (Two-)</meta>

## Transposition of the Consonants.
---

**First Line.**

One observes in these elaborations that the Cabalist <note>A practitioner of numerology or Kabbalistic divination.</note> cannot introduce anything arbitrary into the response. He must adhere to the meaning provided to him by the three numbers of the Trigon <term>Trigon: a triangular grouping of numbers or astrological signs used to derive answers</term> through their symbolic significance. He must express the sense of this meaning using the consonants he is given. Here too he is restricted; for he can use no foreign letters, and of those that lie before him, he must omit none.

Of course, he must possess knowledge of the subjects about which he is asked, provided he wishes to attain the fame of having answered nothing inappropriately—and from this, one recognizes that the symbolic <term>theory of numbers</term> <note>original: "Zahlenlehre"; here referring to the mystical study of numerical values in letters</note> or <term>Cabalistic science</term> <note>original: "gabalistische Wissenschaft"; a system of Jewish mysticism used here to decode hidden meanings</note> was a means of imparting extensive knowledge to students and, so to speak, providing a general overview of all sciences and their principles. One may call the method currently presented an arithmetical game that can provide delight in solitude, sharpens the intellect, and reveals possibilities. It is nevertheless always pleasant to see how the <term>Cabalist</term> <note>original: "Gabalist"; a practitioner of these interpretive arts</note> here draws the truth from a deep well. Six individual consonants in a single line yield expanded sentences of 17 complete words, as was seen in the fourth example in the second line.

1992 Point of the greatest growth of the Assyrian Empire. How it grew, Hebrew history remains entirely silent on this —

Moses says: That Abimelech, a king of Gerar, gave Abraham a thousand pieces of silver coin; Abraham, on the other hand, purchased a rock tomb to serve as his family's burial place for 400 shekels <note>The "shekel" was an ancient unit of weight and later currency in the Near East.</note>. There was, therefore, already coinage among merchants that represented the value of goods — and there were three types of coinage. Small silver pieces, shekels, and "lambs" of silver <note>The "lambs" likely refer to the *kesitah*, an ancient unit of money mentioned in the Bible (e.g., Genesis 33:19), which some traditions interpret as a coin stamped with the image of a lamb.</note>. How many thousands of years does a wild nation likely need to grow from a few pairs of people to the number where it finds it necessary to mint coins and designate them as representatives of goods?

### Third △ <gloss>Trine</gloss> Aereus <note>Latin: "Airy." This refers to a group of three zodiac signs belonging to the element of air: Gemini, Libra, and Aquarius.</note>, or Airy △ <gloss>Trine</gloss>  Gemini ♊ Libra ♎ Aquarius ♒

| Before Christ’s Birth. | ♄ <gloss>Saturn</gloss> ♃ <gloss>Jupiter</gloss> | ♄ <gloss>Saturn</gloss> ♃ <gloss>Jupiter</gloss> |

---

**1921** Semiramis ascended the Assyrian throne. Court de Gebelin <note>Antoine Court de Gebelin (1725–1784) was a French scholar who interpreted ancient history and mythology as symbols of human progress and agricultural cycles.</note> considers her a symbol of the art of government, and likewise Ninus I, and five successive regents, each of whom was intended to teach only a part of the art of government. Ninus II followed to the throne. His empire was already populated with millions of people, and trade with India is said to have been in its highest bloom even then. Generally, writers do not agree in identifying the kings of the Assyrian Empire, and it is therefore difficult to establish their exact number. Up to this point, there were three <term>Saecula<gloss>centuries or long historical epochs</gloss></term> of growth for the aforementioned Assyrian Empire.

**1841** Jacob, the son of Abraham and progenitor of the twelve tribes of Israel, received the name Israel after he had wrestled with the angel of God.

**1826** Abraham died, aged 175 years; from the death of Abraham until the birth of Christ, 1820 years have passed; will another 1820 years likely pass from the birth of Christ until the "general transformation"? <note>The author is reflecting on historical cycles. If 1820 years passed between Abraham and Christ, he wonders if the year 1820 AD—roughly the time of this manuscript—will mark another great turning point in history.</note> — On the other hand, from the creation of the world until the death of Abraham, 1934 years passed, assuming the birth of Christ has been unerringly set in the year 3760 according to the Jewish reckoning <note>This refers to the Anno Mundi calendar used in Hebrew tradition.</note>.

<summary>This page correlates the reigns of legendary Assyrian rulers like Semiramis with biblical history, specifically the life of Jacob and the death of Abraham. The author uses these dates to speculate on 1,820-year historical cycles ending in his own era.</summary>
<keywords>Semiramis, Ninus, Assyrian Empire, Jacob, Abraham, chronology, astrological trines, Saturn-Jupiter conjunctions, Court de Gebelin</keywords>

1000 years before the birth of Christ, Solomon built the Temple in Jerusalem and provided it with a roof of gold, upon which lightning rods were attached <note>The author refers to a historical theory that the golden spikes on the Temple's roof described by Josephus acted as early lightning conductors.</note>; for Mount Zion was heavily exposed to thunderstorms, but from then on, no lightning struck there anymore.

989 the Tyrians <note>Inhabitants of Tyre, a major Phoenician city-state.</note>, whose trade was already flourishing and who had invented glassmaking, sent a colony to Cadiz <note>original: "Cadix"; a city in southwestern Spain founded by the Phoenicians.</note>.

979 Solomon died; his empire was divided into the Kingdom of Judah and the Kingdom of Israel.

890 Carthage transformed itself into a republic.

870 The Assyrian Empire is divided into the Median, Assyrian, and Babylonian [Empires].

850 The Median Empire grew.

811 Beginning of the Macedonian Empire by Caranus the Heraclid <note>A legendary descendant of Hercules and the mythical founder of the Argead dynasty.</note>. End of the Babylonian Empire, for it fell back under the yoke of the Assyrians.

483 The Romans made advances in Greece against Xerxes, the King of the Persians. <note>While Xerxes famously invaded Greece in 480 BCE, the author here suggests Roman involvement or synchronicity during this era of Persian expansion.</note>

453 The Romans in Italy conquered Trident <note>Likely Tridentum, modern-day Trento</note>, and became preeminent over their neighbors.

433 Marks the beginning of the Peloponnesian War between the Athenians and the Lacedaemonians <note>Spartans</note>. It lasted 27 years.

423 Agis <note>Agis II, a Spartan king</note> overcame the Athenians; Nicias <note>original: *Nicas*</note>, their Admiral, terrified by a solar eclipse, neglected to withdraw the Athenian fleet from Syracuse. Consequently, the defeat of the Athenians followed. <note>This refers to the disastrous Sicilian Expedition where a lunar eclipse—noted here as a solar eclipse—was interpreted as an ill omen by Nicias, leading to a fatal delay.</note>

...[Alexander] defeated Darius in three pitched battles: the victor pushed further, conquered Babylon and Egypt, and built Alexandria. By establishing this port, he intended to connect Indian commerce with European commerce; he died without having yet trained a student in his great system. The growth of the Greek monarchy dated from him. At his death, the decline of this empire also began.

324. Alexander's death occurred. His conquests were divided among his generals, who served as governors of the conquered provinces and had secured the loyalty of the troops standing under their command.

294. War of the Romans against the Samnites <note>An ancient Italic people living in Samnium; the Samnite Wars were crucial in Rome's expansion in Italy.</note>.

274. King Pyrrhus invaded Macedonia with war.

264. The first war of the Romans against the Carthaginians <note>The start of the First Punic War (264–241 BC).</note> began.

244. The Romans, as was reminded above, were not seafarers; they had to build ships to wage war against the Carthaginians. They went on board, and their admiral Regulus was beaten, defeated, and dragged into captivity. Yet this first unfortunate attempt did not crush the courage of the brave nation; they wanted to learn to triumph over the elements and tried everything.

In the year 1780, Maria Theresa died after the most glorious reign. Joseph II, her son and Emperor, ascended the throne and abolished <term>serfdom</term> <note>Leibeigenschaft; a condition of debt bondage and legal subjection of peasants to land-owning nobles</note> in his states. He reduced the number of monks, increased the number of dioceses and parishes, was visited in Vienna by the Pope <note>Pope Pius VI visited Vienna in 1782 in a failed attempt to persuade Joseph II to retract his religious reforms</note>, forbade <term>appeals to Rome</term> <note>Rekurs nach Rom; the practice of asking the Papal court to overturn local ecclesiastical or legal decisions</note> in matters of <term>dispensations</term> <note>Dispenssachen; exemptions from church laws, such as marriage restrictions</note>, and prohibited the importation of foreign goods. He raised the population in his states to 25 million. May God grant him a long reign. <note>The author writes in the present tense, suggesting this manuscript was composed during Joseph II's reign, which ended in 1790.</note>

Triple Alliance. In the year 1718, a Quadruple Alliance <note>An alliance between Great Britain, France, the Holy Roman Empire, and the Dutch Republic to counter Spanish expansion.</note> was formed; at Passarowitz, peace was concluded with the Turks <note>The Treaty of Passarowitz (1718) ended the Austro-Turkish War.</note>. In the year 1721, the Congress of Cambrai <note>A diplomatic meeting intended to resolve territorial disputes in Italy following the War of the Quadruple Alliance.</note> took place. 1725: Vienna and Hanoverian Alliances. 1733: Third Spanish War <note>Likely referring to the War of the Polish Succession (1733–1735), in which Spain was heavily involved in efforts to regain Italian lands.</note>. 1735: Peace concluded at Vienna <note>The Preliminary Treaty of Vienna, which largely ended the fighting of the aforementioned war.</note>.

<summary>This page records the astrological "Earthly Trigon" and correlates the movements of Saturn and Jupiter with major European diplomatic treaties and conflicts between 1718 and 1735.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, Saturn, Jupiter, Earthly Trigon, Quadruple Alliance, Treaty of Passarowitz, Congress of Cambrai, Treaty of Vienna, 18th-century diplomacy</keywords>
